It’s a psychological necessity.\n\nSo how do we make progress feel visible especially on days when it’s not?\n\nUse a “Progress Ritual.”\n\n→ At the end of the day, pause. → Write down 3 small ways you moved forward. → That’s it.\nNo fanfare. Just ritual.\nThis works because we rarely notice our progress in real time.\n\nIt gets buried under busyness, meetings, and mental noise.\n\nThe act of looking back gives your brain the reward it needs to keep going.\n\nMomentum builds from meaning.\n\nNow let’s add some comedy.\n\nYoung Jerry Seinfeld had one goal: write new material every day.\n\nTo stay on track, he created a brilliant system.\n\nEach day he wrote, he put a big red X on his calendar.\n\nSoon, a chain of Xs formed.\n\nAnd here’s the key:\n\nDon’t break the chain.\n\nOne red X becomes two. Two becomes ten. Ten becomes identity.\n\nWhether you’re writing, coding, or training\n\nDaily action + visual chain = long-term motivation.\nSummary: The Two-Part Motivation System\nFrom Harvard: Record 3 ways you made progress each day. From Seinfeld: Mark an X for each day you show up then don’t break the chain.\n\nProgress fuels purpose. Consistency fuels confidence.\n\nApply both and you’ll stay on track especially on the tough days.\n\nBecause when your days get better, your weeks get better. When your weeks get better, your months get better. The project she had been leading for 18 months was suddenly reporting into someone else.\n\nShe didn’t mess up. \nShe wasn’t underperforming. \nShe just wasn’t \"there\".\n\nNot at the executive offsite. \nNot at the Friday “golf and growth” circle. \nNot at the CEO’s birthday dinner her male peer casually got invited to.\n\nShe was busy being excellent. \nThey were busy being bonded.\n\n🍷 When she asked her boss about the change, he was surprised: \n“You’re usually aligned with the bigger picture, so we assumed it’d be fine.”\n\nIn Workplace politic-ish:\n\nYumi was predictable. Available. Yet not powerful enough to be consulted.\n\n🔍 What actually happened here?\nWomen are told to build relationships. \nMen build alliances.\nWomen maintain connections. \nMen maintain relevance in power circles.\n\nIt’s not about how many people like you. \nIt’s about how many people speak your name when you’re not in the room.\n\nAnd in most companies, the real decisions - about budget, headcount, succession, are made off-the-clock and off-the-record.\n\n📌 So, how do you stop getting edited out of influence?\nTry these:\n1. 𝗧𝗿𝗮𝗰𝗸 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗿𝗲𝗮𝗹 𝗽𝗼𝘄𝗲𝗿 𝗺𝗮𝗽. \n   Not the org chart. The whisper network / shadow organistion. \n   Who gets invited to early product reviews? \n   Who influences without title? \n   Start mapping that!\n   \n2. 𝗔𝘂𝗱𝗶𝘁 𝘆𝗼𝘂𝗿 𝗻𝗮𝗺𝗲-𝗱𝗿𝗼𝗽 𝗰𝗼𝘂𝗻𝘁. \n   If your name hasn’t been mentioned by 3 different people in senior leadership this month, you are invisible to power, even if you’re a top performer.\n   \n3. 𝗥𝗲𝗱𝗲𝗳𝗶𝗻𝗲 𝗻𝗲𝘁𝘄𝗼𝗿𝗸𝗶𝗻𝗴. \n   Skip the webinars and female empowerment panels. \n   Start showing up where strategy happens: QBRs, investor briefings, offsite planning, cross-functional war rooms.\n   \n4. 𝗖𝗿𝗲𝗮𝘁𝗲 𝘆𝗼𝘂𝗿 𝗼𝘄𝗻 𝗯𝗮𝗰𝗸𝗰𝗵𝗮𝗻𝗻𝗲𝗹. \n   Schedule recurring 1:1s with lateral stakeholders, not to “catch up,” but to co-build. Influence travels faster across than up.\n   \n5. 𝗕𝗲 𝘄𝗵𝗲𝗿𝗲 𝗮𝗯𝘀𝗲𝗻𝗰𝗲 𝗵𝘂𝗿𝘁𝘀. \n   If you vanished for 2 weeks and no one noticed, you’re not central enough to promote.\n   \n🧨 If any of this feels raw, it’s because it is.\nBrilliant women are being rewritten out of their own stories, not for lack of performance, but for lack of positioning.\n\nThat’s why Uma, Grace and I created \n\n👊 𝗙𝗿𝗼𝗺 𝗢𝘂𝘁𝘀𝗶𝗱𝗲𝗿 𝘁𝗼 𝗜𝗻𝘀𝗶𝗱𝗲𝗿: 𝗠𝗮𝘀𝘁𝗲𝗿 𝗪𝗼𝗿𝗸𝗽𝗹𝗮𝗰𝗲 𝗣𝗼𝗹𝗶𝘁𝗶𝗰𝘀👊 \n\nA course for women who are done watching strategic mediocrity rise while they wait for recognition.\n\nIt’s not about becoming someone else. \nIt’s about learning the rules that were never designed for Management","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/top-content/project-management/"},"position":2,"publisher":{"@type":"Organization","name":"LinkedIn"},"sharedContent":{"@type":"WebPage"},"url":"https://www.linkedin.com/posts/jingjin-liu_they-didnt-even-cc-me-this-was-how-activity-7348128185558999041-WGRE"},{"@type":"SocialMediaPosting","articleBody":"Stuck in an endless loop of client changes?\nLost track of what revision this constitutes?\n\nYeah. Been there. Done that.\n\nThe secret? It's not about saying no.\nIt's about saying yes to the right things upfront.\n\nEvery project that goes sideways starts the same way:\nVague agreements. Fuzzy boundaries. Good intentions.\nSix weeks later you're bleeding money and everyone's frustrated.\n\nHere's my framework after 30 years of running two 8-figure businesses:\n\nThe SOW is your salvation.\nNot some boilerplate template. \nA real document that covers:\n• Exact deliverables (not \"design work\" but \"3 homepage concepts, 2 rounds of revisions\")\n• Hours of operation (\"We respond M-F, 9-5 PST. Weekend requests get Monday responses\")\n• Revision rounds spelled out (\"Round 1 includes up to 5 changes. Round 2 includes 3.\")\n• Feedback cycles defined (\"48-hour turnaround for client feedback or the project may be delayed or additional fees may be incurred\")\n\nBut here's what most people miss—\nDon't work on client notes immediately.\n\nClient sends 37 pieces of feedback at 11pm Friday?\nProducer sends conflicting notes from the CEO?\nMarketing wants one thing, sales wants another?\n\nStop.\nCollect everything first.\nResolve the conflicts. Get on the phone and discuss it with your client to get alignment.\nSeparate the \"have to haves\" from the \"nice to haves\".\nThen present unified changes.\n\n\"Based on all feedback received, here are the 8 changes we'll implement. This constitutes revision round 2 of 3.\"\n\nWatch how fast the random requests stop.\nNo extra work that goes unappreciated.\nNo more feelings of being taken advantage of.\n\nCommunicate before the crisis, prevents the crisis from happening.\n\n\"Just so you know, we're entering round 2. You have one more included. After that, it's $X per additional round.\"\n\nNo surprises.\nNo awkward money conversations.\nNo resentment.\n\nScope creep isn't a them problem.\nIt's a you problem.\nAnd that's good news, because that means you are in control.\n\nThey're not trying to take advantage.\nThey just don't know where the boundaries are because you never drew them.\n\nDraw the lines early.\nCommunicate them clearly.\nEveryone wins.\n\nWhat's your most painful scope creep story?\nWhat boundary would've prevented it?\n\nSmall Business Builders\n\n#projectmanagement #clientmanagement #businessgrowth","author":{"@type":"Person","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/C5603AQGlRgwYa_fmMw/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/0/1614101985585?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=5F2u6U4UAnAFAEC13FfopsNhRJzYoFqBuD-n2dKZ-G8"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"https://schema.org/FollowAction","userInteractionCount":630389},"name":"Chris Management","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/top-content/project-management/"},"position":3,"publisher":{"@type":"Organization","name":"LinkedIn"},"sharedContent":{"@type":"WebPage","headline":""},"url":"https://www.linkedin.com/posts/thechrisdo_stuck-in-an-endless-loop-of-client-changes-activity-7396257461151813632-HwqX"},{"@type":"SocialMediaPosting","articleBody":"Last week, I described four design patterns for AI agentic workflows that I believe will drive significant progress: Reflection, Tool use, Planning and Multi-agent collaboration. Instead of having an LLM generate its final output directly, an agentic workflow prompts the LLM multiple times, giving it opportunities to build step by step to higher-quality output. Here, I'd like to discuss Reflection. It's relatively quick to implement, and I've seen it lead to surprising performance gains. \n\nYou may have had the experience of prompting ChatGPT/Claude/Gemini, receiving unsatisfactory output, delivering critical feedback to help the LLM improve its response, and then getting a better response. What if you automate the step of delivering critical feedback, so the model automatically criticizes its own output and improves its response? This is the crux of Reflection. \n\nTake the task of asking an LLM to write code. We can prompt it to generate the desired code directly to carry out some task X. Then, we can prompt it to reflect on its own output, perhaps as follows:\n\nHere’s code intended for task X:\n[previously generated code]\nCheck the code carefully for correctness, style, and efficiency, and give constructive criticism for how to improve it.\n\nSometimes this causes the LLM to spot problems and come up with constructive suggestions. Next, we can prompt the LLM with context including (i) the previously generated code and (ii) the constructive feedback, and ask it to use the feedback to rewrite the code. This can lead to a better response. Repeating the criticism/rewrite process might yield further improvements. This self-reflection process allows the LLM to spot gaps and improve its output on a variety of tasks including producing code, writing text, and answering questions.\n\nAnd we can go beyond self-reflection by giving the LLM tools that help evaluate its output; for example, running its code through a few unit tests to check whether it generates correct results on test cases or searching the web to double-check text output. Then it can reflect on any errors it found and come up with ideas for improvement.\n\nFurther, we can implement Reflection using a multi-agent framework. I've found it convenient to create two agents, one prompted to generate good outputs and the other prompted to give constructive criticism of the first agent's output. The resulting discussion between the two agents leads to improved responses.\n\nReflection is a relatively basic type of agentic workflow, but I've been delighted by how much it improved my applications’ results. If you’re interested in learning more about reflection, I recommend:\n- Self-Refine: Iterative Refinement with Self-Feedback, by Madaan et al. (2023)\n- Reflexion: Language Agents with Verbal Reinforcement Learning, by Shinn et al. (2023)\n- CRITIC: Large Language Models Can Self-Correct with Tool-Interactive Critiquing, by Gou et al. (2024)\n\n[Original text: https://lnkd.in/g4bTuWtU ]","author":{"@type":"Person","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/C5603AQF8paxRmnuJxg/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/0/1517556809594?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=ifkAkH2mPMM3EyHGYRABwsIcoSZH8ZNSKWM9PrWWmG4"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"https://schema.org/FollowAction","userInteractionCount":2604643},"name":"Andrew Ng","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/in/andrewyng"},"datePublished":"2024-03-28T16:55:18.979Z","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/C5603AQF8paxRmnuJxg/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/0/1517556809594?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=ifkAkH2mPMM3EyHGYRABwsIcoSZH8ZNSKWM9PrWWmG4"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":4183},"isPartOf":{"@type":"CollectionPage","name":"Project Management","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/top-content/project-management/"},"position":4,"publisher":{"@type":"Organization","name":"LinkedIn"},"sharedContent":{"@type":"WebPage"},"url":"https://www.linkedin.com/posts/andrewyng_one-agent-for-many-worlds-cross-species-activity-7179159130325078016-_oXr"},{"@type":"SocialMediaPosting","articleBody":"If You Can’t Explain Budgeting Like This, You’re Not Ready for FP&A Interviews.\n\nLet’s assume I ask you the budget for fuel (petrol/diesel) expenses that you are going to incur next year in 2026. How would you budget using the below techniques:\n\n1. Incremental / Traditional Budgeting\nYou take into account the expenses on fuel you made this year. Assuming that amount is INR 50,000. Considering inflation, fuel price changes, and usage patterns, you estimate a 20% increase. Accordingly, your fuel budget for next year will be INR 60,000 (50,000 + 20%)\n\n2. Zero-Based Budgeting\nInstead of taking current year’s expenses, you start from scratch. You estimate how much your car will travel next year. Then factor in expected fuel price and mileage of your vehicle. Based on this, you calculate a reasonable estimate of fuel expenses for next year\n\n3. Activity-Based Budgeting\nLet’s say you use the car only to commute to and from office. For each round trip, your car consumes fuel worth INR 500. Your budgeting would be based on this activity (number of trips taken in a year). If you go to office twice a week, total trips = 52 × 2 = 104. Hence, your fuel budget = 500 × 104 = INR 52,000.\n\n4. Flexible Budgeting\nYour fuel cost depends on how frequently you travel. Instead of one fixed budget, you prepare multiple scenarios. Example:\n2 days/week → INR 52,000\n4 days/week → INR 104,000\nYour actual budget will depend on actual usage during the year.\n\n5. Rolling (Continuous) Budgeting\nYou don’t fix the budget once for the entire year. You keep revising it periodically (monthly/quarterly). Example: if fuel prices increase mid-year or your travel increases, you update the remaining budget accordingly.\n\n6. Top-Down vs Bottom-Up Budgeting\nTop-Down: You decide a cap (say INR 55,000) and adjust your usage to stay within it\nBottom-Up: You calculate expected usage (like ABB/ZBB) and arrive at the number logically\n\n7. Value Proposition Budgeting (using the same example)\nInstead of focusing only on cost, you evaluate whether the expense creates value. You analyse each type of travel:\nOffice commute - necessary\nLeisure / unnecessary trips - optional\nYou may reduce or eliminate low-value trips, carpool, or use alternative transport. Hence, your budget is driven by value derived rather than just estimated usage.\n\nThis way, the same fuel expense can give you very different budgets depending on the approach you use.","author":{"@type":"Person","image":"","interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"https://schema.org/FollowAction","userInteractionCount":131531},"name":"Gaurav Sharma","url":"https://in.linkedin.com/in/gaurav-sharma-gaurav"},"datePublished":"2026-04-29T12:00:07.015Z","image":"","interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":640},"isPartOf":{"@type":"CollectionPage","name":"Project Management","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/top-content/project-management/"},"position":5,"publisher":{"@type":"Organization","name":"LinkedIn"},"sharedContent":{"@type":"WebPage"},"url":"https://www.linkedin.com/posts/gaurav-sharma-gaurav_if-you-cant-explain-budgeting-like-this-activity-7455224394571976704-emIr"},{"@type":"SocialMediaPosting","articleBody":"𝗧𝗼𝗱𝗮𝘆, 𝗣𝗠𝗜 𝗿𝗲𝗹𝗲𝗮𝘀𝗲𝘀 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗳𝗶𝗿𝘀𝘁 𝗿𝗲𝘀𝘂𝗹𝘁𝘀 𝗳𝗿𝗼𝗺 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗹𝗮𝗿𝗴𝗲𝘀𝘁 𝘀𝘁𝘂𝗱𝘆 𝘄𝗲’𝘃𝗲 𝗲𝘃𝗲𝗿 𝗰𝗼𝗻𝗱𝘂𝗰𝘁𝗲𝗱 - 𝗼𝗻 𝗮 𝘁𝗼𝗽𝗶𝗰 𝘁𝗵𝗮𝘁 𝗶𝘀 𝗰𝗿𝗶𝘁𝗶𝗰𝗮𝗹 𝘁𝗼 𝗼𝘂𝗿 𝗽𝗿𝗼𝗳𝗲𝘀𝘀𝗶𝗼𝗻: 𝗣𝗿𝗼𝗷𝗲𝗰𝘁 𝗦𝘂𝗰𝗰𝗲𝘀𝘀.\n\n📚 Read the report: https://lnkd.in/ekRmSj_h\n\nWith this report, we are introducing a simple and scalable way to measure project success. A successful project is one that 𝗱𝗲𝗹𝗶𝘃𝗲𝗿𝘀 𝘃𝗮𝗹𝘂𝗲 𝘄𝗼𝗿𝘁𝗵 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗲𝗳𝗳𝗼𝗿𝘁 𝗮𝗻𝗱 𝗲𝘅𝗽𝗲𝗻𝘀𝗲, as perceived by key stakeholders.\n\nThis clearly represents a shift for our profession, where beyond execution excellence we also feel accountable for doing anything in our power to improve the impact of our work and the value it generates at large.\n\nThe implications for project professionals can be summarized in a framework for delivering 𝗠𝗢𝗥𝗘 success:\n\n📚𝗠anage Perceptions\n\nFor a project to be considered successful, the key stakeholders - customers, executives, or others - must perceive that the project’s outcomes provide sufficient value relative to the perceived investment of resources.\n\n📚𝗢wn Project Success beyond Project Management Success\n\nProject professionals need to take any opportunity to move beyond literal mandates and feel accountable for improving outcomes while minimizing waste.\n\n📚𝗥elentlessly Reassess Project Parameters\n\nProject professionals need to recognize the reality of inevitable and ongoing change, and continuously, in collaboration with stakeholders, reassess the perception of value and adjust plans.\n\n📚𝗘xpand Perspective\n\nAll projects have impacts beyond just the scope of the project itself. Even if we do not control all parameters, we must consider the broader picture and how the project fits within the larger business, goals, or objectives of the enterprise, and ultimately, our world.\n\nI believe executives will be excited about this work. It highlights the value project professionals can bring to their organizations and clarifies the vital role they play in driving transformation, delivering business results, and positively impacting the world. The shift in mindset will encourage project professionals to consider the perceptions of all stakeholders- not just the c-suite, but also customers and communities.\n\nTo deliver more successful projects, business leaders must create environments that empower project professionals. They need to involve them in defining - and continuously reassessing and challenging - project value. Leverage their expertise. Invest in their work. And hold them accountable for contributing to maximize the perception of project value at all phases of the project - beyond excellence in execution.\n\n📚 Please read the report, reflect on its findings, and share it broadly. And comment!\n\nProject Management Institute\n\n#ProjectSuccess #PMI #Leadership #ProjectManagementToday","author":{"@type":"Person","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/D4E03AQGDo7Br_vd2Eg/profile-displayphoto-scale_200_200/B4EZvYdEd4HIAY-/0/1768863059704?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=itVoC_9_A6Qg7pBaBss1HLOsvpqpVnP6MbY_FcerWKE"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"https://schema.org/FollowAction","userInteractionCount":88288},"name":"Pierre Le Manh","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/in/pierre-le-manh-3a4158"},"datePublished":"2024-12-10T02:06:43.387Z","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/D4E03AQGDo7Br_vd2Eg/profile-displayphoto-scale_200_200/B4EZvYdEd4HIAY-/0/1768863059704?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=itVoC_9_A6Qg7pBaBss1HLOsvpqpVnP6MbY_FcerWKE"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":1927},"isPartOf":{"@type":"CollectionPage","name":"Project Management","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/top-content/project-management/"},"position":6,"publisher":{"@type":"Organization","name":"LinkedIn"},"sharedContent":{"@type":"WebPage","headline":""},"url":"https://www.linkedin.com/posts/pierre-le-manh-3a4158_%F0%9D%97%A7%F0%9D%97%BC%F0%9D%97%B1%F0%9D%97%AE%F0%9D%98%86-%F0%9D%97%A3%F0%9D%97%A0%F0%9D%97%9C-%F0%9D%97%BF%F0%9D%97%B2%F0%9D%97%B9%F0%9D%97%B2%F0%9D%97%AE%F0%9D%98%80%F0%9D%97%B2%F0%9D%98%80-%F0%9D%98%81%F0%9D%97%B5%F0%9D%97%B2-activity-7272069190012833792-737M"},{"@type":"SocialMediaPosting","articleBody":"WEF's Global Risks Report 2026 is out 👉 (https://lnkd.in/eaMrdW67).. I put the findings in a 20-year perspective. I mapped 20 years of risk rankings. Two patterns stand out. Both troubling.\n\nThe headline findings in this report: \n🔵 geoeconomic confrontation is now the #1 risk in the short term, \n🔵 economic risks are spiking, \n🔵 50% of experts expect a turbulent or stormy outlook over the next two years.\n\nBut the deeper signal only appears when you track the rankings over time (what I did, see 👇 ).\n\n⚫ 𝐏𝐚𝐭𝐭𝐞𝐫𝐧 𝟏 – 𝐋𝐨𝐧𝐠-𝐭𝐞𝐫𝐦 𝐫𝐢𝐬𝐤𝐬 𝐦𝐢𝐠𝐫𝐚𝐭𝐞 𝐢𝐧𝐭𝐨 𝐭𝐡𝐞 𝐬𝐡𝐨𝐫𝐭 𝐭𝐞𝐫𝐦\nNot overnight. Not mechanically. But persistently.\n\nIn 2007–2010, short-term risks were concrete and immediate: asset bubbles, oil shocks, chronic diseases.\n\nFast forward to today. The long-term top risks for 2026 are:\n 🌪️ extreme weather\n 🌍 biodiversity loss\n 🧠 misinformation\n 🤖 adverse AI outcomes\n\nWhat changed is not that economic risks disappeared.  It’s that structural risks began to act as crisis amplifiers. Extreme weather didn’t replace financial shocks, it reshaped them. Climate risks first entered the short-term top 5 around 2014. By 2020, climate action failure topped the list.\n“Tomorrow’s risks” became today’s stress multipliers, and increasingly, direct crisis drivers. The future didn’t wait.\n\n⚫𝐏𝐚𝐭𝐭𝐞𝐫𝐧 𝟐: 𝐍𝐚𝐭𝐮𝐫𝐞 𝐢𝐬 𝐛𝐞𝐢𝐧𝐠 𝐟𝐨𝐫𝐠𝐨𝐭𝐭𝐞𝐧, 𝐚𝐠𝐚𝐢𝐧\nThis year, environmental risks dropped sharply in the short-term rankings.\nMore worrying:  their severity scores also declined in absolute terms.\n\nYet over the 10-year horizon, environmental risks dominate the top 10.\n\nTwenty years of WEF risk data tell the same story: we consistently recognise long-term environmental threats, then consistently deprioritise them when short-term pressures mount. It's not that we don't know. It's that our attention economy is structurally biased toward the urgent over the important.\n\nThe most interconnected risk for the second year running? Inequality (👇). It fuels everything else: polarisation, migration, political instability, resistance to climate policy.\n\nPerhaps that's where to start: 𝐢𝐟 𝐰𝐞 𝐰𝐚𝐧𝐭 𝐭𝐨 𝐚𝐝𝐝𝐫𝐞𝐬𝐬 𝐥𝐨𝐧𝐠-𝐭𝐞𝐫𝐦 𝐜𝐡𝐚𝐥𝐥𝐞𝐧𝐠𝐞𝐬, 𝐰𝐞 𝐧𝐞𝐞𝐝 𝐭𝐨 𝐫𝐞𝐝𝐮𝐜𝐞 𝐭𝐡𝐞 𝐬𝐡𝐨𝐫𝐭-𝐭𝐞𝐫𝐦 𝐝𝐞𝐬𝐩𝐞𝐫𝐚𝐭𝐢𝐨𝐧 𝐭𝐡𝐚𝐭 𝐤𝐞𝐞𝐩𝐬 𝐮𝐬 𝐭𝐫𝐚𝐩𝐩𝐞𝐝 𝐢𝐧 𝐜𝐫𝐢𝐬𝐢𝐬 𝐦𝐨𝐝𝐞.\n\n#GlobalRisks #WEF #ClimateChange #Sustainability #SystemChange","author":{"@type":"Person","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/D4E03AQGDoAda1TROqg/profile-displayphoto-scale_200_200/B4EZpE7Lm9KsAY-/0/1762092956524?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=Mj8kqwsQynW7P-HhIP_0ggZ7kiTRTjc2Q0nJGoGpD-g"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"https://schema.org/FollowAction","userInteractionCount":77731},"name":"Hans Stegeman","url":"https://nl.linkedin.com/in/hans-stegeman"},"datePublished":"2026-01-15T18:09:14.802Z","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/D4E03AQGDoAda1TROqg/profile-displayphoto-scale_200_200/B4EZpE7Lm9KsAY-/0/1762092956524?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=Mj8kqwsQynW7P-HhIP_0ggZ7kiTRTjc2Q0nJGoGpD-g"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":311},"isPartOf":{"@type":"CollectionPage","name":"Project Management","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/top-content/project-management/"},"position":7,"publisher":{"@type":"Organization","name":"LinkedIn"},"sharedContent":{"@type":"WebPage"},"url":"https://www.linkedin.com/posts/hans-stegeman_wefs-global-risks-report-2026-is-out-activity-7417628951105277952-_I4G"},{"@type":"SocialMediaPosting","articleBody":"Should you try Google’s famous “20% time” experiment to encourage innovation? We tried this at Duolingo years ago. It didn’t work. \n\nIt wasn’t enough time for people to start meaningful projects, and very few people took advantage of it because the framework was pretty vague. I knew there had to be other ways to drive innovation at the company.\n\nSo, here are 3 other initiatives we’ve tried, what we’ve learned from each, and what we're going to try next.\n\n💡 Innovation Awards: Annual recognition for those who move the needle with boundary-pushing projects. \n\nThe upside: These awards make our commitment to innovation clear, and offer a well-deserved incentive to those who have done remarkable work. \n\nThe downside: It’s given to individuals, but we want to incentivize team work. What’s more, it’s not necessarily a framework for coming up with the next big thing.\n\n💻 Hackathon: This is a good framework, and lots of companies do it. Everyone (not just engineers) can take two days to collaborate on and present anything that excites them, as long as it advances our mission or addresses a key business need.\n\nThe upside: Some of our biggest features grew out of hackathon projects, from the Duolingo English Test (born at our first hackathon in 2013) to our avatar builder. \n\nThe downside: Other than the time/resource constraint, projects rarely align with our current priorities. The ones that take off hit the elusive combo of right time + a problem that no other team could tackle. \n\n💥 Special Projects: Knowing that ideal equation, we started a new program for fostering innovation, playfully dubbed DARPA (Duolingo Advanced Research Project Agency). The idea: anyone can pitch an idea at any time. If they get consensus on it and if it’s not in the purview of another team, a cross-functional group is formed to bring the project to fruition. \n\nThe most creative work tends to happen when a problem is not in the clear purview of a particular team; this program creates a path for bringing these kinds of interdisciplinary ideas to life. Our Duo and Lily mascot suits (featured often on our social accounts) came from this, as did our Duo plushie and the merch store. (And if this photo doesn't show why we needed to innovate for new suits, I don't know what will!)\n\nThe biggest challenge: figuring out how to transition ownership of a successful project after the strike team’s work is done. \n\n👀 What’s next? We’re working on a program that proactively identifies big picture, unassigned problems that we haven’t figured out yet and then incentivizes people to create proposals for solving them. How that will work is still to be determined, but we know there is a lot of fertile ground for it to take root.\n\nHow does your company create an environment of creativity that encourages true innovation? I'm interested to hear what's worked for you, so please feel free to share in the comments!\n\n#duolingo #innovation #hackathon #creativity #bigideas","author":{"@type":"Person","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/D4E03AQGpko4nDuw_2w/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/0/1704314906999?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=Ik5UJNVdv_l5ciW8SjHjxbMOjjXJTD98CTbVeOyO9Pg"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"https://schema.org/FollowAction","userInteractionCount":46575},"name":"Severin Hacker","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/in/severinhacker"},"datePublished":"2024-04-16T16:30:06.370Z","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/D4E03AQGpko4nDuw_2w/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/0/1704314906999?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=Ik5UJNVdv_l5ciW8SjHjxbMOjjXJTD98CTbVeOyO9Pg"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":4773},"isPartOf":{"@type":"CollectionPage","name":"Project Management","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/top-content/project-management/"},"position":8,"publisher":{"@type":"Organization","name":"LinkedIn"},"sharedContent":{"@type":"WebPage"},"url":"https://www.linkedin.com/posts/severinhacker_should-you-try-googles-famous-20-time-activity-7186038155458879488-YUf0"},{"@type":"SocialMediaPosting","articleBody":"💎 60 UX Strategy Methods And Activities (Figma) (https://lnkd.in/eCDU-vhR), a large repository of UX methods, templates and activities for ideation sessions and product sprints, from storyboards and brainwriting to 6 thinking hats, journey mapping and concept testing. Neatly put together in one single place by fine folks at Merck.\n\nThe team has also put together a very thorough overview of their UX Strategy Kit (https://lnkd.in/ek5dEYn4), broken down by categories for strategy, observation, ideation and warm-up, along with detailed video walkthroughs, examples and step-by-step guides.\n\nFrankly, most of these methods are unfamiliar to me. And by no means is the point to actually study and apply all of them. What works for you works for you. To strategize, I rely on How Might We but also think about metrics that should be moved once we implement some features or refine some user flows.\n\nFor event storming and brainstorming, I tend to rely on Bono’s 6 thinking hats to align brainstorming, and (of course) journey mapping. For ideation, I love using storyboards to jump right into the user’s success story, but would also use card sorting with cut-out paper cards to understand user’s mental model.\n\nAnd for almost every project, I’d run concept testing with tree testing or Kano model, or low-fidelity/paper prototyping to understand if we are on the right track. Once you sprinkle a bit of critical thinking, early user testing and strategic planning across the design work, you gain confidence that you are moving in the right direction. And really that’s all you need.\n\nA few of my personal bookmarks with UX methods and activities:\n\nUX Tools For Better Thinking, by Adam Amran 👏🏽\nhttps://untools.co/\n\nPlaybook For Universal Design (+ PDF/Powerpoint templates)\nhttps://lnkd.in/ernris4g\n\nUX Methods & Projects, by Vernon Fowler\nhttps://lnkd.in/eAHaiaSm\n\n18F Method Cards\nhttps://methods.18f.gov/\n\nHyperisland UX Methods Resource Kit 👍\nhttps://lnkd.in/eDTaci7T\n\nHow To Design Better UX Workshops, by Slava Shestopalov\nhttps://lnkd.in/edxqCC-n\n\nHow To Run UX Workshops With Users, by yours truly\nhttps://lnkd.in/ejm7_TsS\n\nHappy designing, everyone — I hope you’ll find these guides and resources helpful to get started. Just don’t feel like you have to try out all of them. It might be much more worthwhile to get early feedback from stakeholders and end users, even if your work isn’t really “good” enough. Good luck!\n\n#ux #design","author":{"@type":"Person","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/C4D03AQGcaIGsIO3SBg/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/0/1575521386767?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=b0G9W_ASJZRgTUYQvPYXf_QVLzPB2tfxtJ_1mO0KVlA"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"https://schema.org/FollowAction","userInteractionCount":232490},"name":"Vitaly Friedman","url":"https://de.linkedin.com/in/vitalyfriedman"},"datePublished":"2024-09-23T12:19:30.130Z","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/C4D03AQGcaIGsIO3SBg/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/0/1575521386767?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=b0G9W_ASJZRgTUYQvPYXf_QVLzPB2tfxtJ_1mO0KVlA"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":1001},"isPartOf":{"@type":"CollectionPage","name":"Project Management","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/top-content/project-management/"},"position":9,"publisher":{"@type":"Organization","name":"LinkedIn"},"sharedContent":{"@type":"WebPage"},"url":"https://www.linkedin.com/posts/vitalyfriedman_60-ux-strategy-methods-and-activities-activity-7243957147431047169-RMEP"},{"@type":"SocialMediaPosting","articleBody":"Just created this comprehensive Pandas cheatsheet that I wish I had when I started my  journey!\n\nAfter seeing fellow practitioners struggle with the same pandas operations, I decided to create a simple yet powerful reference guide: \"9 Must-Know Pandas Operations for Working with Data\"\n\nThis is - \n• Focused on real-world use cases, not just syntax\n• Includes time-saving tips I learned the hard way\n• Covers both basic and advanced features\n• Clean, visual layout for quick reference\n\nKey sections include:\n- Data Import/Export tricks\n- Efficient data selection methods\n- Statistical operations\n- Time series handling\n- String manipulation\n- Advanced features you might not know about\n\nPerfect for:\n• Data Professionals (Data Engineers, Data Scientists, ML Engineer, AI Engineers, and Data Analysts)\n• Tech Professionals working with Data\n\nHere are a few other commands that can help you with advanced operations -\n\n1. 𝗗𝗮𝘁𝗮 𝗠𝗮𝗻𝗶𝗽𝘂𝗹𝗮𝘁𝗶𝗼𝗻 𝗦𝗲𝗰𝘁𝗶𝗼𝗻\n   - 𝚙𝚍.𝚌𝚘𝚗𝚌𝚊𝚝() for combining DataFrames\n   - 𝚙𝚒𝚟𝚘𝚝 vs 𝚞𝚗𝚜𝚝𝚊𝚌𝚔 operations\n   - 𝚍𝚏.𝚛𝚎𝚗𝚊𝚖𝚎() for column renaming\n   - 𝚍𝚏.𝚜𝚎𝚝_𝚒𝚗𝚍𝚎𝚡() and 𝚍𝚏.𝚛𝚎𝚜𝚎𝚝_𝚒𝚗𝚍𝚎𝚡()\n\n2. 𝗦𝘁𝗮𝘁𝗶𝘀𝘁𝗶𝗰𝗮𝗹 𝗢𝗽𝗲𝗿𝗮𝘁𝗶𝗼𝗻𝘀\n   - 𝚍𝚏.𝚙𝚌𝚝_𝚌𝚑𝚊𝚗𝚐𝚎() for percentage changes\n   - 𝚍𝚏.𝚌𝚞𝚖𝚜𝚞𝚖(), 𝚍𝚏.𝚌𝚞𝚖𝚙𝚛𝚘𝚍() for cumulative operations\n   - 𝚍𝚏.𝚛𝚊𝚗𝚔() for ranking values\n\n3. 𝗧𝗶𝗺𝗲 𝗦𝗲𝗿𝗶𝗲𝘀\n   - 𝚙𝚍.𝚝𝚘_𝚍𝚊𝚝𝚎𝚝𝚒𝚖𝚎() for converting to datetime\n   - More datetime accessors like .𝚍𝚝.𝚖𝚘𝚗𝚝𝚑, .𝚍𝚝.𝚢𝚎𝚊𝚛\n   - Business day operations with 𝚙𝚍.𝚘𝚏𝚏𝚜𝚎𝚝𝚜\n\n4. 𝗔𝗱𝘃𝗮𝗻𝗰𝗲𝗱 𝗙𝗲𝗮𝘁𝘂𝗿𝗲𝘀\n   - 𝚙𝚍.𝚌𝚞𝚝() and 𝚙𝚍.𝚚𝚌𝚞𝚝() for binning\n   - 𝚙𝚍.𝚐𝚎𝚝_𝚍𝚞𝚖𝚖𝚒𝚎𝚜() for one-hot encoding\n   - Window functions beyond .𝚛𝚘𝚕𝚕𝚒𝚗𝚐()\n   - Cross-tabulation with 𝚙𝚍.𝚌𝚛𝚘𝚜𝚜𝚝𝚊𝚋()\n\n5. 𝗗𝗮𝘁𝗮 𝗖𝗹𝗲𝗮𝗻𝗶𝗻𝗴\n   - 𝚍𝚞𝚙𝚕𝚒𝚌𝚊𝚝𝚎𝚍() method\n   - 𝚍𝚏.𝚠𝚑𝚎𝚛𝚎() and 𝚍𝚏.𝚖𝚊𝚜𝚔()\n   - 𝚍𝚏.𝚌𝚕𝚒𝚙() for limiting values\n\n6.   <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        attributed-text-segment-list__content-truncated" dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">Want to stay motivated every single day?

Borrow a strategy from Harvard.

Then borrow another from stand up comedy.

Together, they’re a powerhouse for momentum, motivation, and mastery.

Here’s how it works:

Let’s start with Harvard.

Researcher Teresa Amabile studied 12,000 daily work diaries across 8 companies.

She wanted to know: What truly motivates people on a day to day basis?

What she found changed how we understand drive.

The #1 driver of daily motivation wasn’t:

Money
Praise
Perks

It was progress.

The days people made progress on meaningful work were the days they felt the best.

Progress isn’t a luxury. It’s a psychological necessity.

So how do we make progress feel visible especially on days when it’s not?

Use a “Progress Ritual.”

→ At the end of the day, pause. → Write down 3 small ways you moved forward. → That’s it.
No fanfare. Just ritual.
This works because we rarely notice our progress in real time.

It gets buried under busyness, meetings, and mental noise.

The act of looking back gives your brain the reward it needs to keep going.

Momentum builds from meaning.

Now let’s add some comedy.

Young Jerry Seinfeld had one goal: write new material every day.

To stay on track, he created a brilliant system.

Each day he wrote, he put a big red X on his calendar.

Soon, a chain of Xs formed.

And here’s the key:

Don’t break the chain.

One red X becomes two. Two becomes ten. Ten becomes identity.

Whether you’re writing, coding, or training

Daily action + visual chain = long-term motivation.
Summary: The Two-Part Motivation System
From Harvard: Record 3 ways you made progress each day. From Seinfeld: Mark an X for each day you show up then don’t break the chain.

Progress fuels purpose. Consistency fuels confidence.

Apply both and you’ll stay on track especially on the tough days.

Because when your days get better, your weeks get better. When your weeks get better, your months get better. When your months get better, your life gets better.

It starts with one small win today.</p>

    <a href="/uas/login?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Fposts%2Fjingjin-liu_they-didnt-even-cc-me-this-was-how-activity-7348128185558999041-WGRE&amp;trk=keyword-landing-page_ellipsis-menu-semaphore-sign-in-redirect&amp;guestReportContentType=POST&amp;_f=guest-reporting" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page_ellipsis-menu-semaphore-sign-in-redirect" data-tracking-will-navigate data-item-type="semaphore" data-semaphore-content-type="POST" data-semaphore-content-urn="urn:li:activity:7348128185558999041" data-semaphore-tracking-prefix="keyword-landing-page_ellipsis-menu-semaphore" data-is-logged-in="false" data-modal="semaphore__toggle" class="semaphore__toggle visited:text-color-text-secondary ellipsis-menu__semaphore ellipsis-menu__item-button flex items-center w-full p-1 cursor-pointer font-sans text-sm font-bold link-styled focus:link-styled link:no-underline active:bg-color-background-container-tint focus:bg-color-background-container-tint hover:bg-color-background-container-tint outline-offset-[-2px]" role="menuitem">

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        attributed-text-segment-list__content-truncated" dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">🎣 “They didn’t even cc me.”
This was how Yumi, a senior marketing director, found out her billion-dollar product had been repositioned, without her input. The project she had been leading for 18 months was suddenly reporting into someone else.

She didn’t mess up. 
She wasn’t underperforming. 
She just wasn’t "there".

Not at the executive offsite. 
Not at the Friday “golf and growth” circle. 
Not at the CEO’s birthday dinner her male peer casually got invited to.

She was busy being excellent. 
They were busy being bonded.

🍷 When she asked her boss about the change, he was surprised: 
“You’re usually aligned with the bigger picture, so we assumed it’d be fine.”

In Workplace politic-ish:

Yumi was predictable. Available. Yet not powerful enough to be consulted.

🔍 What actually happened here?
Women are told to build relationships. 
Men build alliances.
Women maintain connections. 
Men maintain relevance in power circles.

It’s not about how many people like you. 
It’s about how many people speak your name when you’re not in the room.

And in most companies, the real decisions - about budget, headcount, succession, are made off-the-clock and off-the-record.

📌 So, how do you stop getting edited out of influence?
Try these:
1. 𝗧𝗿𝗮𝗰𝗸 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗿𝗲𝗮𝗹 𝗽𝗼𝘄𝗲𝗿 𝗺𝗮𝗽. 
   Not the org chart. The whisper network / shadow organistion. 
   Who gets invited to early product reviews? 
   Who influences without title? 
   Start mapping that!
   
2. 𝗔𝘂𝗱𝗶𝘁 𝘆𝗼𝘂𝗿 𝗻𝗮𝗺𝗲-𝗱𝗿𝗼𝗽 𝗰𝗼𝘂𝗻𝘁. 
   If your name hasn’t been mentioned by 3 different people in senior leadership this month, you are invisible to power, even if you’re a top performer.
   
3. 𝗥𝗲𝗱𝗲𝗳𝗶𝗻𝗲 𝗻𝗲𝘁𝘄𝗼𝗿𝗸𝗶𝗻𝗴. 
   Skip the webinars and female empowerment panels. 
   Start showing up where strategy happens: QBRs, investor briefings, offsite planning, cross-functional war rooms.
   
4. 𝗖𝗿𝗲𝗮𝘁𝗲 𝘆𝗼𝘂𝗿 𝗼𝘄𝗻 𝗯𝗮𝗰𝗸𝗰𝗵𝗮𝗻𝗻𝗲𝗹. 
   Schedule recurring 1:1s with lateral stakeholders, not to “catch up,” but to co-build. Influence travels faster across than up.
   
5. 𝗕𝗲 𝘄𝗵𝗲𝗿𝗲 𝗮𝗯𝘀𝗲𝗻𝗰𝗲 𝗵𝘂𝗿𝘁𝘀. 
   If you vanished for 2 weeks and no one noticed, you’re not central enough to promote.
   
🧨 If any of this feels raw, it’s because it is.
Brilliant women are being rewritten out of their own stories, not for lack of performance, but for lack of positioning.

That’s why <a class="link" href="https://sg.linkedin.com/in/umathana?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Uma</a>, <a class="link" href="https://sg.linkedin.com/in/graceclapham?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Grace</a> and I created 

👊 𝗙𝗿𝗼𝗺 𝗢𝘂𝘁𝘀𝗶𝗱𝗲𝗿 𝘁𝗼 𝗜𝗻𝘀𝗶𝗱𝗲𝗿: 𝗠𝗮𝘀𝘁𝗲𝗿 𝗪𝗼𝗿𝗸𝗽𝗹𝗮𝗰𝗲 𝗣𝗼𝗹𝗶𝘁𝗶𝗰𝘀👊 

A course for women who are done watching strategic mediocrity rise while they wait for recognition.

It’s not about becoming someone else. 
It’s about learning the rules that were never designed for us, and playing like you intend to win.

🔗 Get it if you’re ready, link in comment.

Or wait until they “assume you’d be aligned,” too.</p>

    <article class="relative px-2 pb-0 bg-color-background-container

      </div>

      
            
            
  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        attributed-text-segment-list__content-truncated" dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">Stuck in an endless loop of client changes?
Lost track of what revision this constitutes?

Yeah. Been there. Done that.

The secret? It's not about saying no.
It's about saying yes to the right things upfront.

Every project that goes sideways starts the same way:
Vague agreements. Fuzzy boundaries. Good intentions.
Six weeks later you're bleeding money and everyone's frustrated.

Here's my framework after 30 years of running two 8-figure businesses:

The SOW is your salvation.
Not some boilerplate template. 
A real document that covers:
• Exact deliverables (not "design work" but "3 homepage concepts, 2 rounds of revisions")
• Hours of operation ("We respond M-F, 9-5 PST. Weekend requests get Monday responses")
• Revision rounds spelled out ("Round 1 includes up to 5 changes. Round 2 includes 3.")
• Feedback cycles defined ("48-hour turnaround for client feedback or the project may be delayed or additional fees may be incurred")

But here's what most people miss—
Don't work on client notes immediately.

Client sends 37 pieces of feedback at 11pm Friday?
Producer sends conflicting notes from the CEO?
Marketing wants one thing, sales wants another?

Stop.
Collect everything first.
Resolve the conflicts. Get on the phone and discuss it with your client to get alignment.
Separate the "have to haves" from the "nice to haves".
Then present unified changes.

"Based on all feedback received, here are the 8 changes we'll implement. This constitutes revision round 2 of 3."

Watch how fast the random requests stop.
No extra work that goes unappreciated.
No more feelings of being taken advantage of.

Communicate before the crisis, prevents the crisis from happening.

"Just so you know, we're entering round 2. You have one more included. After that, it's $X per additional round."

No surprises.
No awkward money conversations.
No resentment.

Scope creep isn't a them problem.
It's a you problem.
And that's good news, because that means you are in control.

They're not trying to take advantage.
They just don't know where the boundaries are because you never drew them.

Draw the lines early.
Communicate them clearly.
Everyone wins.

What's your most painful scope creep story?
What boundary would've prevented it?

<a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/showcase/small-business-builders/?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Small Business Builders</a>

</p>

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        attributed-text-segment-list__content-truncated" dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">Last week, I described four design patterns for AI agentic workflows that I believe will drive significant progress: Reflection, Tool use, Planning and Multi-agent collaboration. Instead of having an LLM generate its final output directly, an agentic workflow prompts the LLM multiple times, giving it opportunities to build step by step to higher-quality output. Here, I'd like to discuss Reflection. It's relatively quick to implement, and I've seen it lead to surprising performance gains. 

You may have had the experience of prompting ChatGPT/Claude/Gemini, receiving unsatisfactory output, delivering critical feedback to help the LLM improve its response, and then getting a better response. What if you automate the step of delivering critical feedback, so the model automatically criticizes its own output and improves its response? This is the crux of Reflection. 

Take the task of asking an LLM to write code. We can prompt it to generate the desired code directly to carry out some task X. Then, we can prompt it to reflect on its own output, perhaps as follows:

Here’s code intended for task X:
[previously generated code]
Check the code carefully for correctness, style, and efficiency, and give constructive criticism for how to improve it.

Sometimes this causes the LLM to spot problems and come up with constructive suggestions. Next, we can prompt the LLM with context including (i) the previously generated code and (ii) the constructive feedback, and ask it to use the feedback to rewrite the code. This can lead to a better response. Repeating the criticism/rewrite process might yield further improvements. This self-reflection process allows the LLM to spot gaps and improve its output on a variety of tasks including producing code, writing text, and answering questions.

And we can go beyond self-reflection by giving the LLM tools that help evaluate its output; for example, running its code through a few unit tests to check whether it generates correct results on test cases or searching the web to double-check text output. Then it can reflect on any errors it found and come up with ideas for improvement.

Further, we can implement Reflection using a multi-agent framework. I've found it convenient to create two agents, one prompted to generate good outputs and the other prompted to give constructive criticism of the first agent's output. The resulting discussion between the two agents leads to improved responses.

Reflection is a relatively basic type of agentic workflow, but I've been delighted by how much it improved my applications’ results. If you’re interested in learning more about reflection, I recommend:
- Self-Refine: Iterative Refinement with Self-Feedback, by Madaan et al. (2023)
- Reflexion: Language Agents with Verbal Reinforcement Learning, by Shinn et al. (2023)
- CRITIC: Large Language Models Can Self-Correct with Tool-Interactive Critiquing, by Gou et al. (2024)

[Original text: <a class="link" href="https://lnkd.in/g4bTuWtU?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>https://lnkd.in/g4bTuWtU</a> ]</p>

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        attributed-text-segment-list__content-truncated" dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">If You Can’t Explain Budgeting Like This, You’re Not Ready for FP&amp;A Interviews.

Let’s assume I ask you the budget for fuel (petrol/diesel) expenses that you are going to incur next year in 2026. How would you budget using the below techniques:

1. Incremental / Traditional Budgeting
You take into account the expenses on fuel you made this year. Assuming that amount is INR 50,000. Considering inflation, fuel price changes, and usage patterns, you estimate a 20% increase. Accordingly, your fuel budget for next year will be INR 60,000 (50,000 + 20%)

2. Zero-Based Budgeting
Instead of taking current year’s expenses, you start from scratch. You estimate how much your car will travel next year. Then factor in expected fuel price and mileage of your vehicle. Based on this, you calculate a reasonable estimate of fuel expenses for next year

3. Activity-Based Budgeting
Let’s say you use the car only to commute to and from office. For each round trip, your car consumes fuel worth INR 500. Your budgeting would be based on this activity (number of trips taken in a year). If you go to office twice a week, total trips = 52 × 2 = 104. Hence, your fuel budget = 500 × 104 = INR 52,000.

4. Flexible Budgeting
Your fuel cost depends on how frequently you travel. Instead of one fixed budget, you prepare multiple scenarios. Example:
2 days/week → INR 52,000
4 days/week → INR 104,000
Your actual budget will depend on actual usage during the year.

5. Rolling (Continuous) Budgeting
You don’t fix the budget once for the entire year. You keep revising it periodically (monthly/quarterly). Example: if fuel prices increase mid-year or your travel increases, you update the remaining budget accordingly.

6. Top-Down vs Bottom-Up Budgeting
Top-Down: You decide a cap (say INR 55,000) and adjust your usage to stay within it
Bottom-Up: You calculate expected usage (like ABB/ZBB) and arrive at the number logically

7. Value Proposition Budgeting (using the same example)
Instead of focusing only on cost, you evaluate whether the expense creates value. You analyse each type of travel:
Office commute - necessary
Leisure / unnecessary trips - optional
You may reduce or eliminate low-value trips, carpool, or use alternative transport. Hence, your budget is driven by value derived rather than just estimated usage.

This way, the same fuel expense can give you very different budgets depending on the approach you use.</p>

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        attributed-text-segment-list__content-truncated" dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">𝗧𝗼𝗱𝗮𝘆, 𝗣𝗠𝗜 𝗿𝗲𝗹𝗲𝗮𝘀𝗲𝘀 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗳𝗶𝗿𝘀𝘁 𝗿𝗲𝘀𝘂𝗹𝘁𝘀 𝗳𝗿𝗼𝗺 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗹𝗮𝗿𝗴𝗲𝘀𝘁 𝘀𝘁𝘂𝗱𝘆 𝘄𝗲’𝘃𝗲 𝗲𝘃𝗲𝗿 𝗰𝗼𝗻𝗱𝘂𝗰𝘁𝗲𝗱 - 𝗼𝗻 𝗮 𝘁𝗼𝗽𝗶𝗰 𝘁𝗵𝗮𝘁 𝗶𝘀 𝗰𝗿𝗶𝘁𝗶𝗰𝗮𝗹 𝘁𝗼 𝗼𝘂𝗿 𝗽𝗿𝗼𝗳𝗲𝘀𝘀𝗶𝗼𝗻: 𝗣𝗿𝗼𝗷𝗲𝗰𝘁 𝗦𝘂𝗰𝗰𝗲𝘀𝘀.

📚 Read the report: <a class="link" href="https://lnkd.in/ekRmSj_h?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>https://lnkd.in/ekRmSj_h</a>

With this report, we are introducing a simple and scalable way to measure project success. A successful project is one that 𝗱𝗲𝗹𝗶𝘃𝗲𝗿𝘀 𝘃𝗮𝗹𝘂𝗲 𝘄𝗼𝗿𝘁𝗵 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗲𝗳𝗳𝗼𝗿𝘁 𝗮𝗻𝗱 𝗲𝘅𝗽𝗲𝗻𝘀𝗲, as perceived by key stakeholders.

This clearly represents a shift for our profession, where beyond execution excellence we also feel accountable for doing anything in our power to improve the impact of our work and the value it generates at large.

The implications for project professionals can be summarized in a framework for delivering 𝗠𝗢𝗥𝗘 success:

📚𝗠anage Perceptions

For a project to be considered successful, the key stakeholders - customers, executives, or others - must perceive that the project’s outcomes provide sufficient value relative to the perceived investment of resources.

📚𝗢wn Project Success beyond Project Management Success

Project professionals need to take any opportunity to move beyond literal mandates and feel accountable for improving outcomes while minimizing waste.

📚𝗥elentlessly Reassess Project Parameters

Project professionals need to recognize the reality of inevitable and ongoing change, and continuously, in collaboration with stakeholders, reassess the perception of value and adjust plans.

📚𝗘xpand Perspective

All projects have impacts beyond just the scope of the project itself. Even if we do not control all parameters, we must consider the broader picture and how the project fits within the larger business, goals, or objectives of the enterprise, and ultimately, our world.

I believe executives will be excited about this work. It highlights the value project professionals can bring to their organizations and clarifies the vital role they play in driving transformation, delivering business results, and positively impacting the world. The shift in mindset will encourage project professionals to consider the perceptions of all stakeholders- not just the c-suite, but also customers and communities.

To deliver more successful projects, business leaders must create environments that empower project professionals. They need to involve them in defining - and continuously reassessing and challenging - project value. Leverage their expertise. Invest in their work. And hold them accountable for contributing to maximize the perception of project value at all phases of the project - beyond excellence in execution.

📚 Please read the report, reflect on its findings, and share it broadly. And comment!

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        attributed-text-segment-list__content-truncated" dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">WEF's Global Risks Report 2026 is out 👉 (<a class="link" href="https://lnkd.in/eaMrdW67?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>https://lnkd.in/eaMrdW67</a>).. I put the findings in a 20-year perspective. I mapped 20 years of risk rankings. Two patterns stand out. Both troubling.

The headline findings in this report: 
🔵 geoeconomic confrontation is now the #1 risk in the short term, 
🔵 economic risks are spiking, 
🔵 50% of experts expect a turbulent or stormy outlook over the next two years.

But the deeper signal only appears when you track the rankings over time (what I did, see 👇 ).

⚫ 𝐏𝐚𝐭𝐭𝐞𝐫𝐧 𝟏 – 𝐋𝐨𝐧𝐠-𝐭𝐞𝐫𝐦 𝐫𝐢𝐬𝐤𝐬 𝐦𝐢𝐠𝐫𝐚𝐭𝐞 𝐢𝐧𝐭𝐨 𝐭𝐡𝐞 𝐬𝐡𝐨𝐫𝐭 𝐭𝐞𝐫𝐦
Not overnight. Not mechanically. But persistently.

In 2007–2010, short-term risks were concrete and immediate: asset bubbles, oil shocks, chronic diseases.

Fast forward to today. The long-term top risks for 2026 are:
 🌪️ extreme weather
 🌍 biodiversity loss
 🧠 misinformation
 🤖 adverse AI outcomes

What changed is not that economic risks disappeared.  It’s that structural risks began to act as crisis amplifiers. Extreme weather didn’t replace financial shocks, it reshaped them. Climate risks first entered the short-term top 5 around 2014. By 2020, climate action failure topped the list.
“Tomorrow’s risks” became today’s stress multipliers, and increasingly, direct crisis drivers. The future didn’t wait.

⚫𝐏𝐚𝐭𝐭𝐞𝐫𝐧 𝟐: 𝐍𝐚𝐭𝐮𝐫𝐞 𝐢𝐬 𝐛𝐞𝐢𝐧𝐠 𝐟𝐨𝐫𝐠𝐨𝐭𝐭𝐞𝐧, 𝐚𝐠𝐚𝐢𝐧
This year, environmental risks dropped sharply in the short-term rankings.
More worrying:  their severity scores also declined in absolute terms.

Yet over the 10-year horizon, environmental risks dominate the top 10.

Twenty years of WEF risk data tell the same story: we consistently recognise long-term environmental threats, then consistently deprioritise them when short-term pressures mount. It's not that we don't know. It's that our attention economy is structurally biased toward the urgent over the important.

The most interconnected risk for the second year running? Inequality (👇). It fuels everything else: polarisation, migration, political instability, resistance to climate policy.

Perhaps that's where to start: 𝐢𝐟 𝐰𝐞 𝐰𝐚𝐧𝐭 𝐭𝐨 𝐚𝐝𝐝𝐫𝐞𝐬𝐬 𝐥𝐨𝐧𝐠-𝐭𝐞𝐫𝐦 𝐜𝐡𝐚𝐥𝐥𝐞𝐧𝐠𝐞𝐬, 𝐰𝐞 𝐧𝐞𝐞𝐝 𝐭𝐨 𝐫𝐞𝐝𝐮𝐜𝐞 𝐭𝐡𝐞 𝐬𝐡𝐨𝐫𝐭-𝐭𝐞𝐫𝐦 𝐝𝐞𝐬𝐩𝐞𝐫𝐚𝐭𝐢𝐨𝐧 𝐭𝐡𝐚𝐭 𝐤𝐞𝐞𝐩𝐬 𝐮𝐬 𝐭𝐫𝐚𝐩𝐩𝐞𝐝 𝐢𝐧 𝐜𝐫𝐢𝐬𝐢𝐬 𝐦𝐨𝐝𝐞.

<a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/uas/login?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Ffeed%2Fhashtag%2Fglobalrisks&amp;trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>#GlobalRisks</a> <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/uas/login?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Ffeed%2Fhashtag%2Fwef&amp;trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>#WEF</a> <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/uas/login?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Ffeed%2Fhashtag%2Fclimatechange&amp;trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>#ClimateChange</a> <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/uas/login?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Ffeed%2Fhashtag%2Fsustainability&amp;trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>#Sustainability</a> <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/uas/login?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Ffeed%2Fhashtag%2Fsystemchange&amp;trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>#SystemChange</a></p>

    <a href="/uas/login?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Fposts%2Fseverinhacker_should-you-try-googles-famous-20-time-activity-7186038155458879488-YUf0&amp;trk=keyword-landing-page_ellipsis-menu-semaphore-sign-in-redirect&amp;guestReportContentType=POST&amp;_f=guest-reporting" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page_ellipsis-menu-semaphore-sign-in-redirect" data-tracking-will-navigate data-item-type="semaphore" data-semaphore-content-type="POST" data-semaphore-content-urn="urn:li:activity:7186038155458879488" data-semaphore-tracking-prefix="keyword-landing-page_ellipsis-menu-semaphore" data-is-logged-in="false" data-modal="semaphore__toggle" class="semaphore__toggle visited:text-color-text-secondary ellipsis-menu__semaphore ellipsis-menu__item-button flex items-center w-full p-1 cursor-pointer font-sans text-sm font-bold link-styled focus:link-styled link:no-underline active:bg-color-background-container-tint focus:bg-color-background-container-tint hover:bg-color-background-container-tint outline-offset-[-2px]" role="menuitem">

                      <icon class="ellipsis-menu__item-icon text-color-text h-[24px] w-[24px] mr-1" data-delayed-url="https://static.licdn.com/aero-v1/sc/h/iq0x9q37wj214o129ai1yjut">

                      举报此动态

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        attributed-text-segment-list__content-truncated" dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">Should you try Google’s famous “20% time” experiment to encourage innovation? We tried this at <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/company/duolingo?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Duolingo</a> years ago. It didn’t work. 

It wasn’t enough time for people to start meaningful projects, and very few people took advantage of it because the framework was pretty vague. I knew there had to be other ways to drive innovation at the company.

So, here are 3 other initiatives we’ve tried, what we’ve learned from each, and what we're going to try next.

💡 Innovation Awards: Annual recognition for those who move the needle with boundary-pushing projects. 

The upside: These awards make our commitment to innovation clear, and offer a well-deserved incentive to those who have done remarkable work. 

The downside: It’s given to individuals, but we want to incentivize team work. What’s more, it’s not necessarily a framework for coming up with the next big thing.

💻 Hackathon: This is a good framework, and lots of companies do it. Everyone (not just engineers) can take two days to collaborate on and present anything that excites them, as long as it advances our mission or addresses a key business need.

The upside: Some of our biggest features grew out of hackathon projects, from the <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/company/duolingo-english-test-?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Duolingo English Test</a> (born at our first hackathon in 2013) to our avatar builder. 

The downside: Other than the time/resource constraint, projects rarely align with our current priorities. The ones that take off hit the elusive combo of right time + a problem that no other team could tackle. 

💥 Special Projects: Knowing that ideal equation, we started a new program for fostering innovation, playfully dubbed DARPA (Duolingo Advanced Research Project Agency). The idea: anyone can pitch an idea at any time. If they get consensus on it and if it’s not in the purview of another team, a cross-functional group is formed to bring the project to fruition. 

The most creative work tends to happen when a problem is not in the clear purview of a particular team; this program creates a path for bringing these kinds of interdisciplinary ideas to life. Our Duo and Lily mascot suits (featured often on our social accounts) came from this, as did our Duo plushie and the merch store. (And if this photo doesn't show why we needed to innovate for new suits, I don't know what will!)

The biggest challenge: figuring out how to transition ownership of a successful project after the strike team’s work is done. 

👀 What’s next? We’re working on a program that proactively identifies big picture, unassigned problems that we haven’t figured out yet and then incentivizes people to create proposals for solving them. How that will work is still to be determined, but we know there is a lot of fertile ground for it to take root.

How does your company create an environment of creativity that encourages true innovation? I'm interested to hear what's worked for you, so please feel free to share in the comments!

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        attributed-text-segment-list__content-truncated" dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">💎 60 UX Strategy Methods And Activities (Figma) (<a class="link" href="https://lnkd.in/eCDU-vhR?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>https://lnkd.in/eCDU-vhR</a>), a large repository of UX methods, templates and activities for ideation sessions and product sprints, from storyboards and brainwriting to 6 thinking hats, journey mapping and concept testing. Neatly put together in one single place by fine folks at Merck.

The team has also put together a very thorough overview of their UX Strategy Kit (<a class="link" href="https://lnkd.in/ek5dEYn4?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>https://lnkd.in/ek5dEYn4</a>), broken down by categories for strategy, observation, ideation and warm-up, along with detailed video walkthroughs, examples and step-by-step guides.

Frankly, most of these methods are unfamiliar to me. And by no means is the point to actually study and apply all of them. What works for you works for you. To strategize, I rely on How Might We but also think about metrics that should be moved once we implement some features or refine some user flows.

For event storming and brainstorming, I tend to rely on Bono’s 6 thinking hats to align brainstorming, and (of course) journey mapping. For ideation, I love using storyboards to jump right into the user’s success story, but would also use card sorting with cut-out paper cards to understand user’s mental model.

And for almost every project, I’d run concept testing with tree testing or Kano model, or low-fidelity/paper prototyping to understand if we are on the right track. Once you sprinkle a bit of critical thinking, early user testing and strategic planning across the design work, you gain confidence that you are moving in the right direction. And really that’s all you need.

A few of my personal bookmarks with UX methods and activities:

UX Tools For Better Thinking, by <a class="link" href="https://cz.linkedin.com/in/adamamran?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Adam Amran</a> 👏🏽
<a class="link" href="https://untools.co/?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>https://untools.co/</a>

Playbook For Universal Design (+ PDF/Powerpoint templates)
<a class="link" href="https://lnkd.in/ernris4g?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>https://lnkd.in/ernris4g</a>

UX Methods &amp; Projects, by <a class="link" href="https://au.linkedin.com/in/vfowler?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Vernon Fowler</a>
<a class="link" href="https://lnkd.in/eAHaiaSm?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>https://lnkd.in/eAHaiaSm</a>

18F Method Cards
<a class="link" href="https://methods.18f.gov/?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>https://methods.18f.gov/</a>

Hyperisland UX Methods Resource Kit 👍
<a class="link" href="https://lnkd.in/eDTaci7T?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>https://lnkd.in/eDTaci7T</a>

How To Design Better UX Workshops, by <a class="link" href="https://nl.linkedin.com/in/shestopalov?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Slava Shestopalov</a>
<a class="link" href="https://lnkd.in/edxqCC-n?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>https://lnkd.in/edxqCC-n</a>

How To Run UX Workshops With Users, by yours truly
<a class="link" href="https://lnkd.in/ejm7_TsS?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>https://lnkd.in/ejm7_TsS</a>

Happy designing, everyone — I hope you’ll find these guides and resources helpful to get started. Just don’t feel like you have to try out all of them. It might be much more worthwhile to get early feedback from stakeholders and end users, even if your work isn’t really “good” enough. Good luck!

<a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/uas/login?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Ffeed%2Fhashtag%2Fux&amp;trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>#ux</a> <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/uas/login?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Ffeed%2Fhashtag%2Fdesign&amp;trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>#design</a></p>

    <a href="/uas/login?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Fposts%2Fbrijpandeyji_just-created-this-comprehensive-pandas-cheatsheet-activity-7294812754421424128-DIIm&amp;trk=keyword-landing-page_ellipsis-menu-semaphore-sign-in-redirect&amp;guestReportContentType=POST&amp;_f=guest-reporting" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page_ellipsis-menu-semaphore-sign-in-redirect" data-tracking-will-navigate data-item-type="semaphore" data-semaphore-content-type="POST" data-semaphore-content-urn="urn:li:activity:7294812754421424128" data-semaphore-tracking-prefix="keyword-landing-page_ellipsis-menu-semaphore" data-is-logged-in="false" data-modal="semaphore__toggle" class="semaphore__toggle visited:text-color-text-secondary ellipsis-menu__semaphore ellipsis-menu__item-button flex items-center w-full p-1 cursor-pointer font-sans text-sm font-bold link-styled focus:link-styled link:no-underline active:bg-color-background-container-tint focus:bg-color-background-container-tint hover:bg-color-background-container-tint outline-offset-[-2px]" role="menuitem">

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        attributed-text-segment-list__content-truncated" dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">Just created this comprehensive Pandas cheatsheet that I wish I had when I started my  journey!

After seeing fellow practitioners struggle with the same pandas operations, I decided to create a simple yet powerful reference guide: "9 Must-Know Pandas Operations for Working with Data"

This is - 
• Focused on real-world use cases, not just syntax
• Includes time-saving tips I learned the hard way
• Covers both basic and advanced features
• Clean, visual layout for quick reference

Key sections include:
- Data Import/Export tricks
- Efficient data selection methods
- Statistical operations
- Time series handling
- String manipulation
- Advanced features you might not know about

Perfect for:
• Data Professionals (Data Engineers, Data Scientists, ML Engineer, AI Engineers, and Data Analysts)
• Tech Professionals working with Data

Here are a few other commands that can help you with advanced operations -

1. 𝗗𝗮𝘁𝗮 𝗠𝗮𝗻𝗶𝗽𝘂𝗹𝗮𝘁𝗶𝗼𝗻 𝗦𝗲𝗰𝘁𝗶𝗼𝗻
   - 𝚙𝚍.𝚌𝚘𝚗𝚌𝚊𝚝() for combining DataFrames
   - 𝚙𝚒𝚟𝚘𝚝 vs 𝚞𝚗𝚜𝚝𝚊𝚌𝚔 operations
   - 𝚍𝚏.𝚛𝚎𝚗𝚊𝚖𝚎() for column renaming
   - 𝚍𝚏.𝚜𝚎𝚝_𝚒𝚗𝚍𝚎𝚡() and 𝚍𝚏.𝚛𝚎𝚜𝚎𝚝_𝚒𝚗𝚍𝚎𝚡()

2. 𝗦𝘁𝗮𝘁𝗶𝘀𝘁𝗶𝗰𝗮𝗹 𝗢𝗽𝗲𝗿𝗮𝘁𝗶𝗼𝗻𝘀
   - 𝚍𝚏.𝚙𝚌𝚝_𝚌𝚑𝚊𝚗𝚐𝚎() for percentage changes
   - 𝚍𝚏.𝚌𝚞𝚖𝚜𝚞𝚖(), 𝚍𝚏.𝚌𝚞𝚖𝚙𝚛𝚘𝚍() for cumulative operations
   - 𝚍𝚏.𝚛𝚊𝚗𝚔() for ranking values

3. 𝗧𝗶𝗺𝗲 𝗦𝗲𝗿𝗶𝗲𝘀
   - 𝚙𝚍.𝚝𝚘_𝚍𝚊𝚝𝚎𝚝𝚒𝚖𝚎() for converting to datetime
   - More datetime accessors like .𝚍𝚝.𝚖𝚘𝚗𝚝𝚑, .𝚍𝚝.𝚢𝚎𝚊𝚛
   - Business day operations with 𝚙𝚍.𝚘𝚏𝚏𝚜𝚎𝚝𝚜

4. 𝗔𝗱𝘃𝗮𝗻𝗰𝗲𝗱 𝗙𝗲𝗮𝘁𝘂𝗿𝗲𝘀
   - 𝚙𝚍.𝚌𝚞𝚝() and 𝚙𝚍.𝚚𝚌𝚞𝚝() for binning
   - 𝚙𝚍.𝚐𝚎𝚝_𝚍𝚞𝚖𝚖𝚒𝚎𝚜() for one-hot encoding
   - Window functions beyond .𝚛𝚘𝚕𝚕𝚒𝚗𝚐()
   - Cross-tabulation with 𝚙𝚍.𝚌𝚛𝚘𝚜𝚜𝚝𝚊𝚋()

5. 𝗗𝗮𝘁𝗮 𝗖𝗹𝗲𝗮𝗻𝗶𝗻𝗴
   - 𝚍𝚞𝚙𝚕𝚒𝚌𝚊𝚝𝚎𝚍() method
   - 𝚍𝚏.𝚠𝚑𝚎𝚛𝚎() and 𝚍𝚏.𝚖𝚊𝚜𝚔()
   - 𝚍𝚏.𝚌𝚕𝚒𝚙() for limiting values

6. 𝗠𝗮𝘆𝗯𝗲 𝗮 𝗡𝗲𝘄 𝗦𝗲𝗰𝘁𝗶𝗼𝗻 𝗼𝗻 𝗜𝗻𝗱𝗲𝘅𝗶𝗻𝗴
   - MultiIndex operations
   - Index alignment
   - Cross-section selection with .𝚡𝚜()

Have I overlooked anything? 

Please share your thoughts—your insights are priceless to me.
</p>
